Image name: POGBb_task3_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 15
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are M and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect M from B.

First, I identify all direct connections from the target nodes:
*   Node M is connected to: L, E, J.
*   Node B is connected to: N, C, H, E.

Next, I will systematically identify distinct paths between M and B. To ensure I find the minimum cut, I will look for edge-disjoint paths. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove (edge-cut) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

Let's find edge-disjoint paths from M to B:

1.  **Path 1: M - E - B**
    *   Edges used: (M, E), (E, B)

2.  **Path 2: M - L - E - F - K - G - H - B**
    *   Edges used: (M, L), (L, E), (E, F), (F, K), (K, G), (G, H), (H, B)
    *   Let's check if this path is edge-disjoint from Path 1:
        *   (M, L) is not (M, E) or (E, B).
        *   (L, E) is not (M, E) or (E, B).
        *   (E, F) is not (M, E) or (E, B).
        *   (F, K) is not (M, E) or (E, B).
        *   (K, G) is not (M, E) or (E, B).
        *   (G, H) is not (M, E) or (E, B).
        *   (H, B) is not (M, E) or (E, B).
    *   Path 2 is indeed edge-disjoint from Path 1.

3.  **Path 3: M - J - P - O - N - B**
    *   Edges used: (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N), (N, B)
    *   Let's check if this path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2:
        *   (M, J) is not used in Path 1 or Path 2.
        *   (J, P) is not used in Path 1 or Path 2.
        *   (P, O) is not used in Path 1 or Path 2.
        *   (O, N) is not used in Path 1 or Path 2.
        *   (N, B) is not used in Path 1 or Path 2.
    *   Path 3 is indeed edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.

I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between M and B. This means that at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect M from B.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that achieves this disconnection.
A straightforward approach is to remove all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes, if that number matches the minimum required.

Consider the edges connected to M:
*   (M, L)
*   (M, E)
*   (M, J)

If I remove these three edges, M will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and therefore from B.
Let's verify this cut set:
1.  Removing (M, L) breaks all paths that start with M-L (e.g., M-L-E-B, M-L-E-F-K-G-H-B).
2.  Removing (M, E) breaks all paths that start with M-E (e.g., M-E-B, M-E-F-K-G-H-B).
3.  Removing (M, J) breaks all paths that start with M-J (e.g., M-J-P-O-N-B, M-J-P-I-C-B, M-J-D-P-I-H-B).

Since all paths from M must start with one of these three edges, removing them all ensures no path exists between M and B. This cut set has a size of 3.

Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and I found a cut set of 3 edges, this is the minimum number of edges required.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, L), (M, E), (M, J)]
--- Ground Truth ---

        *   Mark C as visited. `Visited: {I, J, H, C}`.

        *   Enqueue: `(O, [I, P, O])`.
        *   Mark P as visited. `Visited: {I, J, H, C, P}`.

5.  **Level 3:**
    *   Dequeue `(D, [I, J, D])`.
        *   Neighbors of D: J, P. (J, P are visited).
        *   Mark D as visited. `Visited: {I, J, H, C, P, D}`.
    *   Dequeue `(P, [I, J, P])`. (P is already visited).
    *   Dequeue `(M, [I, J, M])`.
        *   Neighbors of M: J, O, E. (J is visited. O is in the queue).
        *   **E is the target node!**
        *   Path found: `[I, J, M, E]`.
        *   Length of path: 3 edges.

Since BFS guarantees that the first time the target node is reached, it is via the shortest path, we can stop here.

I will verify the path:
*   Is I connected to J? Yes.
*   Is J connected to M? Yes.
*   Is M connected to E? Yes.
The path is valid.

Final Answer: Length: 3, Path: [I, J, M, E]

Image name: PG_task2_gt2_PAIR1
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 15
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are N and G. My task is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect N from G. I cannot remove N or G.

First, I will identify all direct neighbors of N that lead to the rest of the graph.
N is connected to B, O, and Y.
Node Y is only connected to N and O. If O is removed, Y becomes isolated from the rest of the graph (except N). Therefore, any path from N through Y must eventually pass through O to reach G. This means Y is not an independent "gateway" from N; it's part of the "O" branch.

So, all paths from N to G must pass through either node B or node O.

Let's test if removing a single node can disconnect N and G:
*   If I remove B, paths through O (e.g., N-O-P-J-I-H-G) would still exist.
*   If I remove O, paths through B (e.g., N-B-E-F-K-G) would still exist.
*   If I remove any other single node (e.g., E, H, F, C, I, J, P, D, M, L, K, X), there would always be alternative paths remaining between N and G. For example, removing H would still leave paths like N-B-E-F-K-G.

Since no single node can disconnect N and G, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

Now, let's consider removing two nodes.
Based on the observation that all paths from N must go through B or O, if I remove both B and O, N will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph.
Let's verify this:
1.  If node B is removed, N can no longer use the N-B connection.
2.  If node O is removed, N can no longer use the N-O connection. Additionally, N's connection to Y is also effectively severed from the rest of the graph because Y is only connected to N and O. Since O is removed, Y becomes a dead end for N.

Therefore, removing both B and O will disconnect N from all other white nodes, and consequently from G. This is a cut set of size 2.

I have found a cut set of size 2, and I have established that a cut set of size 1 is not possible. Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [B, O]
--- Ground Truth ---

    *   Node D (black) is connected to J and P.

2.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    *   To isolate node F, I would need to remove all its incident edges: (F,X), (F,G), (F,K), (F,E). This would be a cut of size 4.
    *   To isolate node D, I would need to remove all its incident edges: (D,J) and (D,P). This would be a cut of size 2.

    *   Since node D has only two connections, (D,J) and (D,P), any path reaching D must use one of these two edges.
    *   If I remove only one edge, say (D,J), a path could still reach D via (D,P). Therefore, at least two edges must be removed to disconnect D from the rest of the graph.

4.  **Verify the chosen cut set:**

    *   Consequently, no path can exist between F and D, as D is completely disconnected.

Image name: POGBa_task2_gt3_PAIR4
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 15
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are H and O. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect H from O. I cannot remove H or O.

2.  **Analyze connections to O:** Node O is directly connected to C, P, Y, and N. Node Y is only connected to O. This means that any path from H to O must eventually pass through C, P, or N. If C, P, and N are all removed, O will only be connected to Y. Since Y is only connected to O, removing C, P, and N would effectively isolate O from the rest of the graph (except Y), making it impossible for H to reach O.

3.  **Propose a cut set:** Based on the observation in step 2, removing the nodes {C, P, N} should disconnect H from O. This set has 3 nodes.

4.  **Verify the proposed cut set {C, P, N}:**
    *   If C, P, and N are removed, O is only connected to Y.
    *   Now, I need to check if H can reach Y.
    *   H's neighbors are G, B, I.
    *   From G: G is connected to X, F, K. None of these nodes (X, F, K) are connected to Y.
    *   From B: B is connected to E. E is connected to M, L, F. None of these nodes (E, M, L, F) are connected to Y.
    *   From I: I is connected to J. J is connected to M. None of these nodes (J, M) are connected to Y.
    *   Since Y is only connected to O, and O is now isolated from all other nodes except Y, H cannot reach O.
    *   Therefore, {C, P, N} is a valid cut set, and its size is 3.

5.  **Determine minimality (check if a cut set of size 2 exists):** To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to show that no set of 2 nodes can disconnect H from O. I will systematically consider pairs of critical nodes.
    *   **Consider paths from H to O:**
        *   Paths through I: H - I - P - O, H - I - C - O, H - I - C - N - O, H - I - J - P - O
        *   Paths through B: H - B - C - O, H - B - C - N - O, H - B - N - O
        *   Paths through G: H - G - X - F - E - M - J - P - O (and similar paths via F or K, all eventually leading to J, which connects to P or I).

    *   **Test pairs of nodes from {I, B, C, P, N} (the most direct connections):**
        *   **{I, B}:**
            *   Paths through I are broken.
            *   Paths through B are broken.
            *   However, paths through G remain: H - G - X - F - E - M - J - P - O. (J is connected to P, and P is not removed). So, {I, B} is not a cut set.
        *   **{I, C}:**
            *   Paths through I are broken.
            *   Paths H - B - C - O and H - B - C - N - O are broken by C.
            *   However, H - B - N - O is still open. Also, paths through G (e.g., H - G - ... - J - P - O) are still open. So, {I, C} is not a cut set.
        *   **{I, P}:**
            *   Paths through I are broken.
            *   Paths through G (e.g., H - G - ... - J - P - O) are broken by P (since J connects to P or I, and both are removed or blocked).
            *   However, paths through B (e.g., H - B - C - O and H - B - N - O) are still open. So, {I, P} is not a cut set.
        *   **{B, C}:**
            *   Paths through B are broken.
            *   Paths H - I - C - O and H - I - C - N - O are broken by C.
            *   However, H - I - P - O is still open. Also, paths through G (e.g., H - G - ... - J - P - O) are still open. So, {B, C} is not a cut set.

            *   Paths through B are broken.
            *   Paths H - I - C - N - O are broken by N.
            *   However, H - I - P - O and H - I - C - O are still open. Also, paths through G (e.g., H - G - ... - J - P - O) are still open. So, {B, N} is not a cut set.

            *   Paths H - I - C - O, H - I - C - N - O, H - B - C - O, H - B - C - N - O are broken by C.
            *   However, H - B - N - O is still open. So, {C, P} is not a cut set.

            *   However, H - I - P - O and H - I - J - P - O are still open. Also, paths through G (e.g., H - G - ... - J - P - O) are still open. So, {C, N} is not a cut set.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E (black) and H (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between E and H.

1.  **Identify all direct connections to the target node H:**
    Node H is directly connected to nodes B, G, and I. This means any path from E to H must pass through one of these three nodes immediately before reaching H. The edges connecting H to the rest of the graph are (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).

2.  **Consider a cut set by removing all edges directly connected to H:**
    If we remove the edges (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H), node H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist from E to H. This set of edges forms a cut set of size 3.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut set exists (i.e., size 1 or 2):**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to show that removing any 1 or 2 edges is insufficient to disconnect E and H. This can be done by finding at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H. If there are `k` edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's theorem, at least `k` edges must be removed to disconnect the two nodes.

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths from E to H:
    *   **Path 1:** E - B - H
        Edges: {(E, B), (B, H)}

    *   **Path 2:** E - F - G - H
        Edges: {(E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}

    *   **Path 3:** E - L - M - F - K - J - I - H
        Edges: {(E, L), (L, M), (M, F), (F, K), (K, J), (J, I), (I, H)}

    Let's verify that these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint:
    *   Path 1 uses (E, B) and (B, H).
    *   Path 2 uses (E, F), (F, G), and (G, H).
    *   Path 3 uses (E, L), (L, M), (M, F), (F, K), (K, J), (J, I), and (I, H).

    Comparing the edge sets:
    *   No edge in Path 1 is present in Path 2.
    *   No edge in Path 1 is present in Path 3.
    *   No edge in Path 2 is present in Path 3 (e.g., Path 2 uses (E,F) while Path 3 uses (E,L); Path 2 uses (F,G) while Path 3 uses (F,K); Path 2 uses (G,H) while Path 3 uses (I,H)).

    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, according to Menger's theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    I have identified a cut set of 3 edges ([(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]) that successfully disconnects E from H. I have also demonstrated, by finding 3 edge-disjoint paths, that a minimum of 3 edges must be removed. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

I will systematically identify edge-disjoint paths from N to H:

    Edges: (N, B), (B, H)

2.  **Path 2: N - O - C - I - H**

3.  **Path 3: N - Y - O - P - D - J - M - L - E - F - K - G - H**

    -   None of these edges are present in Path 2: {(N, O), (O, C), (C, I), (I, H)}.
    Therefore, Path 3 is edge-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.

I have successfully identified three edge-disjoint paths between N and H. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect N and H is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths, which is 3.

To achieve this disconnection, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges. A straightforward way to disconnect a node is to remove all edges directly connected to it.
Node N has three direct connections: (N, Y), (N, O), and (N, B).
If these three edges are removed, node N becomes isolated from the rest of the graph, ensuring no path can exist between N and H. This is a valid cut set of size 3.

    *   Therefore, a single edge removal is not sufficient. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E (black) and J (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from J.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node E is connected to: L, M, F, G, B.
    *   Node J is connected to: M, K, I, P, D.

2.  **Analyze paths to J:**
    All paths from E to J must pass through one of J's direct neighbors. Let's list these neighbors and check if they are reachable from E:
    *   **M:** Reachable from E (e.g., E-M, E-L-M, E-F-M, E-G-F-M).
    *   **K:** Reachable from E (e.g., E-F-K, E-F-X-K, E-G-F-K, E-G-F-X-K).
    *   **I:** Reachable from E (e.g., E-G-H-I, E-B-C-I, E-B-G-H-I, E-B-N-C-I).
    *   **P:** Reachable from E (e.g., E-G-H-I-P, E-B-C-O-P, E-B-N-Y-O-P).
    *   **D:** Node D is only connected to J. There are no paths from E to D. Therefore, the edge (D, J) is not part of any path from E to J, and its removal is not necessary to disconnect E from J.

3.  **Formulate a potential cut set:**
    To disconnect E from J, we must cut all paths leading into J from the reachable neighbors (M, K, I, P). A straightforward way to do this is to remove the edges directly connecting these nodes to J.
    Consider the cut set C = [(M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J)].
    *   Removing (M, J) breaks all paths from E that go through M to J.
    *   Removing (K, J) breaks all paths from E that go through K to J.
    *   Removing (I, J) breaks all paths from E that go through I to J.
    *   Removing (P, J) breaks all paths from E that go through P to J.
    Since all paths from E to J must use one of these four edges, removing them will completely disconnect E from J.
    The size of this cut set is 4.

4.  **Prove minimality using Menger's Theorem (finding edge-disjoint paths):**
    To prove that 4 is the minimum, we need to find 4 edge-disjoint paths between E and J.
    Let's identify four such paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J
        Edges: {(E, M), (M, J)}
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - K - J
        Edges: {(E, F), (F, K), (K, J)}
    *   **Path 3:** E - G - H - I - J
        Edges: {(E, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J)}
    *   **Path 4:** E - B - C - O - P - J
        Edges: {(E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)}

    Let's verify that these paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   The first edges from E are (E, M), (E, F), (E, G), (E, B), which are all distinct.
    *   The last edges to J are (M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J), which are all distinct.
    *   All intermediate edges (e.g., (F, K) in P2, (G, H) in P3, (B, C) in P4) are also distinct and do not overlap between any of these four paths.

    Since we have found 4 edge-disjoint paths between E and J, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect E and J is at least 4.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    We found a cut set of 4 edges that disconnects E from J, and we proved that at least 4 edges are required. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, J and E. This is a minimum cut problem, which, by Menger's theorem, is equivalent to finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between J and E.

First, let's identify all possible paths between J and E.
The direct neighbors of J are D, P, M, I.
The direct neighbors of E are L, B, F, M.

Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths from J to E:

1.  **Path 1: J - M - E**
    Edges used: `(J, M)`, `(M, E)`

2.  **Path 2: J - I - H - F - E**
    Edges used: `(J, I)`, `(I, H)`, `(H, F)`, `(F, E)`
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as it uses different starting edges from J (`(J, I)` vs `(J, M)`) and different ending edges to E (`(F, E)` vs `(M, E)`), and all intermediate edges are distinct.

3.  **Path 3: J - P - O - N - B - E**
    Edges used: `(J, P)`, `(P, O)`, `(O, N)`, `(N, B)`, `(B, E)`
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2. It uses a different starting edge from J (`(J, P)`), a different ending edge to E (`(B, E)`), and all intermediate edges are distinct from those in Path 1 and Path 2.

Now, let's try to find a fourth edge-disjoint path.
The remaining unused edge from J is `(J, D)`. So, any fourth path must start with `J - D`.
From D, we can go to P or O.
*   If `J - D - P`: The edge `(P, O)` is used in Path 3. So, this path cannot be edge-disjoint from Path 3.
*   If `J - D - O`: The edge `(O, N)` is used in Path 3. Alternatively, `O` is connected to `Y`. So, `J - D - O - Y - N - B - E`. Let's check the edges: `(J, D)`, `(D, O)`, `(O, Y)`, `(Y, N)`, `(N, B)`, `(B, E)`.
    *   `(N, B)` is used in Path 3.
    *   `(B, E)` is used in Path 3.
    Therefore, this path is not edge-disjoint from Path 3.

All paths starting with `(J, D)` eventually use an edge already part of Path 3.
The path `J - M - L - E` uses `(J, M)`, which is part of Path 1, so it's not edge-disjoint.

Since we could only find 3 edge-disjoint paths, by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect J and E is 3.

Next, we need to find a cut set of 3 edges that achieves this disconnection.
Let's consider the following set of edges:
1.  `(J, M)`: This edge cuts off all paths from J that go through M. This includes `J - M - E` and `J - M - L - E`.
2.  `(B, E)`: This edge cuts off all paths that reach E via node B. This covers paths like `J - D - P - O - N - B - E`, `J - P - O - N - B - E`, `J - P - O - Y - N - B - E`, and `J - I - H - C - N - B - E`.
3.  `(F, E)`: This edge cuts off all paths that reach E via node F. This covers paths like `J - I - H - F - E`, `J - I - H - G - X - F - E`, and `J - I - H - G - K - F - E`.

Let's verify that removing these three edges completely disconnects J from E:
*   Node E's direct neighbors are M, L, B, F.
*   After removing `(J, M)`, J cannot reach M. Since L is only connected to M and E, J cannot reach L either (as it cannot reach M).
*   After removing `(B, E)`, J cannot reach E via B.
*   After removing `(F, E)`, J cannot reach E via F.

Since all paths from J to M, L, B, and F (which are E's only connections) are broken, J is completely disconnected from E.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(J, M), (B, E), (F, E)]

3.  **Search for Small Cut Sets:**
    *   **Size 1:** I examine the neighbors of E (L, F, G, B) and I (P, H, G, C). Removing any single node (e.g., G, which is a common neighbor) would still leave multiple paths open. For instance, if G is removed, paths like E-B-C-I, E-F-K-H-I, and E-L-M-J-P-I would still exist. Thus, a cut set of size 1 is not possible.
    *   **Size 2:** I try removing two nodes. For example, if I remove G and C, paths like E-L-M-J-P-I and E-F-K-H-I would still connect E and I. Thus, a cut set of size 2 is not possible.
    *   **Size 3:** I try removing three nodes. For example, if I remove G, C, and P, paths like E-F-K-H-I would still connect E and I (since H is still connected to I and reachable from E via F and K). Thus, a cut set of size 3 is not possible.
4.  **Identify Vertex-Disjoint Paths:** Since smaller cut sets are not sufficient, I will look for vertex-disjoint paths between E and I. By Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths.
    *   **Path 1:** E - G - I
        *   Intermediate node: {G}
    *   **Path 2:** E - B - C - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {B, C}

    *   **Path 4:** E - L - M - J - P - I

    *   {G}
    *   {B, C}
    *   {F, K, H}
    *   {L, M, J, P}
    All these sets are indeed disjoint. This confirms that there are at least 4 vertex-disjoint paths between E and I.
6.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:** According to Menger's Theorem, if there are 4 vertex-disjoint paths, then the minimum vertex cut must be at least 4.
7.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 4:** A straightforward way to find a cut set is to remove all direct neighbors of one of the target nodes.
    *   The neighbors of I are P, H, G, C. If I remove these four nodes, I will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from E. None of these nodes are E or I.
    *   Alternatively, the neighbors of E are L, F, G, B. Removing these four nodes would isolate E.
8.  **Conclusion:** Since I found 4 vertex-disjoint paths, and I can identify a cut set of 4 nodes ([P, H, G, C]), this is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Image name: FD-K_task3_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 15
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are B and M. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect B from M.

1.  **Identify all direct connections to the target node M:**
    Node M is directly connected to E, L, and J. This means there are three edges directly connected to M: (E, M), (L, M), and (J, M).

2.  **Consider removing all direct connections to M:**
    If we remove the edges (E, M), (L, M), and (J, M), node M will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Since M is isolated, no path can exist between B and M. This constitutes a cut set of size 3.

3.  **Check if a smaller cut set (e.g., size 2) is possible:**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, we need to show that no set of 2 edges can disconnect B from M.
    Let's consider all possible pairs of edges that could be removed.

    *   **Case 1: Removing two edges directly connected to M.**
        *   If we remove (E, M) and (L, M): The edge (J, M) remains. A path like B - C - I - J - M would still exist.
        *   If we remove (E, M) and (J, M): The edge (L, M) remains. A path like B - H - E - L - M would still exist.
        *   If we remove (L, M) and (J, M): The edge (E, M) remains. A path like B - H - E - M would still exist.
        In all these sub-cases, removing only two direct edges to M is not sufficient to disconnect M from B.

    *   **Case 2: Removing two edges not necessarily directly connected to M.**
        Let's try to identify two "bottleneck" edges further away from M.
        Consider the paths from B to M:
        *   Path 1: B - H - E - M
        *   Path 2: B - C - I - J - M
        *   Path 3: B - N - O - P - J - M
        *   Path 4: B - H - E - L - M
        *   Path 5: B - H - G - F - E - M
        *   And many more variations.

        Let's try to find two edges that would cut all these paths.
        *   If we remove (H, E) and (I, J):
            *   Path 1 (B-H-E-M) is broken by (H, E).
            *   Path 2 (B-C-I-J-M) is broken by (I, J).
            *   However, Path 3 (B-N-O-P-J-M) is still active. Also, Path 4 (B-H-E-L-M) is broken by (H,E), but Path 5 (B-H-G-F-E-M) is still active (it uses (F,E) not (H,E)).
            So, [(H, E), (I, J)] is not a cut set.

        *   If we remove (J, M) and (E, M):
            *   All paths ending in J-M are broken.
            *   All paths ending in E-M are broken.
            *   However, paths ending in L-M are still active. For example, B - H - E - L - M is still active because (E,L) is not cut.
            So, [(J, M), (E, M)] is not a cut set.

    It is evident that there are multiple alternative paths between B and M, making it difficult to find a cut set of size 2. The existence of three distinct "entry points" to M (E, L, J) and the fact that paths can reach these points through various routes (e.g., E can be reached via H or F, J via I or P) strongly suggests that a minimum of 3 edges is required.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    The simplest and most effective way to disconnect M from B is to remove all edges directly connected to M. As shown above, removing any two of these three edges leaves at least one path active. Therefore, all three edges (E, M), (L, M), and (J, M) must be removed.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, M), (L, M), (J, M)]

Let's list node-disjoint paths from H to N:

1.  **Path 1: H - B - N**

    This path is node-disjoint from Path 2 ({I, C} is distinct from {E, M, J, P, O}).

*   Therefore, no path can exist from H to N after removing B, C, and O.

Image name: Tu_task3_gt3_PAIR4
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 15
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between E and J.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node E is connected to: L, M, P, B.
    *   Node J is connected to: M, D, P, I.

2.  **Analyze the role of connected nodes:**
    *   Node D is only connected to J. This means any path from E to J cannot pass through D and then continue to J, as D is a dead-end for any path originating from E. Therefore, the edge (J, D) is not part of any path from E to J that needs to be cut.
    *   The other nodes connected to J (M, P, I) are potential "entry points" for paths from E to J.

3.  **Identify potential cut edges:**
    All paths from E to J must eventually pass through one of the nodes M, P, or I to reach J. Therefore, the edges connecting these nodes to J are critical. These edges are (J, M), (J, P), and (J, I).

4.  **Propose a cut set:**
    If we remove the edges (J, M), (J, P), and (J, I), then J will be disconnected from M, P, and I. Since D is a dead-end for paths from E, J will be completely disconnected from E. This cut set has 3 edges.

5.  **Verify the proposed cut set by checking all paths:**
    *   Path E - M - J: Broken by removing (J, M).
    *   Path E - P - J: Broken by removing (J, P).
    *   Path E - L - M - J: Broken by removing (J, M).
    *   Path E - B - C - I - J: Broken by removing (J, I).
    *   Path E - B - C - H - I - J: Broken by removing (J, I).
    *   Path E - B - O - P - J: Broken by removing (J, P).
    *   Path E - B - C - N - O - P - J: Broken by removing (J, P).
    *   Path E - B - C - N - Y - O - P - J: Broken by removing (J, P).
    *   Path E - P - F - G - H - I - J: Broken by removing (J, I).
    *   Any other path from E to J must eventually use one of the edges (J, M), (J, P), or (J, I) to reach J. Removing these three edges ensures no path remains.

6.  **Determine minimality:**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to find at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J. If such paths exist, then by Menger's theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect E and J.
    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J (Edges: (E, M), (M, J))
    *   **Path 2:** E - P - J (Edges: (E, P), (P, J))
    *   **Path 3:** E - B - C - I - J (Edges: (E, B), (B, C), (C, I), (I, J))

    These three paths are indeed edge-disjoint, meaning they do not share any common edges.
    Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect E and J.
    As we found a cut set of 3 edges, this is the minimum.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(J, M), (J, P), (J, I)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [[['E', 'B'], ['M', 'J'], ['G', 'H']], [['E', 'B'], ['M', 'J'], ['F', 'E']]]}

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 15
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from I. This is a min-cut problem, and by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

First, I will identify all direct neighbors of G and I:
- Node G is connected to: E, F, H, B.
- Node I is connected to: H, J, C, P.

Next, I will attempt to find edge-disjoint paths between G and I. Edge-disjoint means that no two paths share any common edge.

1.  **Path 1: G - H - I**
    *   Edges: (G, H), (H, I)

2.  **Path 2: G - B - C - I**
    *   Edges: (G, B), (B, C), (C, I)
    *   This path uses edges distinct from Path 1.

3.  **Path 3: G - E - M - J - I**
    *   Edges: (G, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, I)
    *   This path uses edges distinct from Path 1 and Path 2.

4.  **Path 4: G - F - K - J - P - I**
    *   Edges: (G, F), (F, K), (K, J), (J, P), (P, I)
    *   Let's verify if these edges are distinct from the previous three paths:
        *   Edges from G: (G, H), (G, B), (G, E), (G, F) are all distinct.
        *   Edges to I: (H, I), (C, I), (J, I), (P, I) are all distinct.
        *   Intermediate edges: (B, C), (E, M), (M, J), (F, K), (K, J), (J, P) are all distinct from each other and from the edges connected to G and I.
    *   Therefore, this is a fourth edge-disjoint path.

Since I have found 4 edge-disjoint paths between G and I, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect G and I is 4.

Now, I need to identify a specific set of 4 edges whose removal achieves this disconnection. A straightforward way to disconnect a node from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges directly connected to it.

Consider node I. It has four direct connections: (H, I), (J, I), (C, I), and (P, I).
If these four edges are removed, node I becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist between G and I.

This set of edges is: [(H, I), (J, I), (C, I), (P, I)].
The number of edges in this cut set is 4, which matches the minimum required number determined by Menger's Theorem.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(H, I), (J, I), (C, I), (P, I)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['F', 'E'], ['G', 'H']]}

Step-by-step reasoning. The two black nodes are labeled Y and L. I will perform a breadth-first search (BFS) starting from node Y to find the shortest path to node L.

1.  **Initialize:**
    *   Queue: `[(Y, [Y])]` (node, path_to_node)
    *   Visited: `{Y}`

2.  **Level 1:**
    *   Dequeue `(Y, [Y])`.
    *   Neighbors of Y: N, O.
    *   Enqueue `(N, [Y, N])`. Mark N as visited.
    *   Enqueue `(O, [Y, O])`. Mark O as visited.
    *   Queue: `[(N, [Y, N]), (O, [Y, O])]`

3.  **Level 2:**
    *   Dequeue `(N, [Y, N])`.
    *   Neighbors of N: Y (visited), O (visited), B, C.
    *   Enqueue `(B, [Y, N, B])`. Mark B as visited.
    *   Enqueue `(C, [Y, N, C])`. Mark C as visited.
    *   Dequeue `(O, [Y, O])`.
    *   Neighbors of O: M, P, Y (visited), N (visited), C (visited).
    *   Enqueue `(M, [Y, O, M])`. Mark M as visited.
    *   Enqueue `(P, [Y, O, P])`. Mark P as visited.
    *   Queue: `[(B, [Y, N, B]), (C, [Y, N, C]), (M, [Y, O, M]), (P, [Y, O, P])]`

4.  **Level 3:**
    *   Dequeue `(B, [Y, N, B])`.
    *   Neighbors of B: N (visited), C (visited), E, F.
    *   Enqueue `(E, [Y, N, B, E])`. Mark E as visited.
    *   Enqueue `(F, [Y, N, B, F])`. Mark F as visited.
    *   Dequeue `(C, [Y, N, C])`.
    *   Neighbors of C: O (visited), N (visited), B (visited), I, H.
    *   Enqueue `(I, [Y, N, C, I])`. Mark I as visited.
    *   Enqueue `(H, [Y, N, C, H])`. Mark H as visited.
    *   Dequeue `(M, [Y, O, M])`.
    *   Neighbors of M: J, L, O (visited).
    *   Enqueue `(J, [Y, O, M, J])`. Mark J as visited.
    *   **L is a neighbor of M!** We have found the target node L.
    *   The path is `[Y, O, M, L]`.
    *   The length of this path is 3 (Y-O, O-M, M-L).

Since BFS guarantees that the first path found to the target node is the shortest, we can conclude that a path of length 3 is the shortest.

Final Answer: Length: 3, Path: [Y, O, M, L]

Image name: PGS_task3_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 15
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect the two black nodes, G and I. This is a minimum s-t cut problem, where s=G and t=I. By Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between G and I.

First, let's identify potential edge-disjoint paths from G to I:

1.  **Path 1 (P1): G - H - I**
    Edges: `(G, H), (H, I)`

2.  **Path 2 (P2): G - K - J - I**
    Edges: `(G, K), (K, J), (J, I)`
    (P1 and P2 are edge-disjoint as they share no common edges.)

3.  **Path 3 (P3): G - F - M - L - E - B - N - O - P - I**
    Edges: `(G, F), (F, M), (M, L), (L, E), (E, B), (B, N), (N, O), (O, P), (P, I)`
    (Let's check if P3 is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2.
    P1 uses (G,H), (H,I). P2 uses (G,K), (K,J), (J,I). P3 uses none of these edges.
    So, P1, P2, and P3 are indeed edge-disjoint.)

We have found 3 edge-disjoint paths. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 3.

Now, let's try to find a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects G and I. If we can find such a set, it will be the minimum.

Consider the following set of 3 edges: `[(H, I), (K, J), (B, N)]`

Let's verify if removing these three edges disconnects G from I:

1.  **Paths through H:**
    *   The direct path `G - H - I` is broken by removing `(H, I)`.

2.  **Paths through K and J:**
    *   Any path from G that goes through K and then to J (e.g., `G - K - J - I`, `G - F - K - J - I`, `G - X - K - J - I`, `G - F - X - K - J - I`) will be broken by removing `(K, J)`.
    *   After `(K, J)` is removed, K cannot reach J, and thus cannot reach I via J or J-P.
    *   K has no other connections to I's neighborhood (C or P) without passing through J or other nodes that lead to B-N.

3.  **Paths through F, X, M, L, E, B, N to C or P:**
    *   Any path from G that goes through F or X and eventually reaches B (e.g., `G - F - M - L - E - B`, `G - F - E - B`, `G - X - F - M - L - E - B`, `G - X - F - E - B`) must then pass through N to reach C or P (i.e., `B - N - C - I` or `B - N - O - P - I`).
    *   Removing `(B, N)` breaks all these paths, ensuring that no path from G can reach C or P via this route.

By removing `(H, I)`, `(K, J)`, and `(B, N)`, all identified paths from G to I are broken.
*   `(H, I)` cuts the direct path through H.
*   `(K, J)` cuts all paths that go through K and then J to reach I (either directly to I or via P).
*   `(B, N)` cuts all paths that go through the larger network (F, M, L, E, B) to reach C or P, and then I.

Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and we found a cut set of 3 edges, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, I), (K, J), (B, N)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['F', 'E'], ['G', 'H']]}

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 15
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E (black) and J (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from J.

1.  **Identify all direct connections to the target node J from the rest of the graph.**
    Node J is directly connected to:
    *   P (via edge (J, P))
    *   D (via edge (J, D))
    *   I (via edge (J, I))
    *   M (via edge (J, M))

2.  **Analyze the relevance of these connections for paths originating from E.**
    *   The node D is only connected to J. There are no paths from E that lead to D. Therefore, the edge (J, D) does not need to be removed to disconnect E from J.
    *   All other direct connections to J (M, I, P) are reachable from E through various paths. This means any path from E to J must eventually pass through M, I, or P to reach J.

3.  **Formulate a strategy based on these critical connections.**
    To ensure no path exists between E and J, we must sever all connections from M, I, and P to J. This can be achieved by removing the edges (J, M), (J, I), and (J, P).

4.  **Verify this cut set:**
    *   Removing (J, M) breaks all paths that go through M to reach J (e.g., E-M-J, E-L-M-J, E-F-G-X-K-M-J).
    *   Removing (J, I) breaks all paths that go through I to reach J (e.g., E-B-C-I-J, E-B-H-I-J, E-F-G-H-I-J).
    *   Removing (J, P) breaks all paths that go through P to reach J (e.g., E-B-C-O-P-J).
    Since all possible paths from E to J must utilize one of these three "entry points" (M, I, or P) to connect to J, removing these three edges guarantees complete disconnection.

5.  **Determine if this is the minimum number of edges.**
    *   Could it be 1 edge? No, because there are clearly multiple independent routes to J (e.g., via M, via I, via P). Removing one edge would leave at least two other routes.
    *   Could it be 2 edges? Let's assume we remove any two of the three critical edges, for example, (J, M) and (J, I). The path E-B-C-O-P-J would still exist, as it uses P to connect to J, and (J, P) would still be intact. Therefore, 2 edges are not sufficient.
    *   Since 1 and 2 edges are not enough, and we found a solution with 3 edges, 3 is the minimum number of edges.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(I, J), (J, M), (J, P)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [[['E', 'B'], ['M', 'J'], ['G', 'H']], [['E', 'B'], ['M', 'J'], ['F', 'E']]]}

Step-by-step reasoning.

1.  **Identify direct neighbors of B:** C, N, O, E, H.
2.  **Identify direct neighbors of M:** L, E, J.

Now, I will systematically trace all possible paths from B to M and identify critical nodes.

**Group 1: Paths through node E**
*   Path 1: B -> E -> M
*   Path 2: B -> E -> L -> M
All paths in this group pass through node E. If E is removed, both of these paths are broken.

**Group 2: Paths not through node E**
These paths must connect B to M via M's other neighbor, J.
To reach J from B, we need to find paths from B to J.
Let's examine the connections from B's neighbors (excluding E) towards J:

*   **From B -> C:**
    *   B -> C -> N -> O -> P -> J -> M
    *   B -> C -> O -> P -> J -> M
    *   B -> C -> H -> I -> P -> J -> M
*   **From B -> N:**
    *   B -> N -> O -> P -> J -> M (also B -> N -> C -> ... which is covered above)
*   **From B -> O:**
    *   B -> O -> P -> J -> M (also B -> O -> C -> ... or B -> O -> N -> ... which are covered above)
*   **From B -> H:**
    *   B -> H -> I -> P -> J -> M (also B -> H -> C -> ... which is covered above)
    *   B -> H -> G -> F -> P -> J -> M

Upon reviewing all paths in Group 2, it is clear that every single path from B to J (and subsequently to M) must pass through node P. From P, the only way to M is P -> J -> M (P-D is a dead end for reaching M). Therefore, to break all paths in Group 2, we must remove either node P or node J.

**Conclusion:**
To completely disconnect B and M, we must break all paths in Group 1 AND all paths in Group 2.
*   To break Group 1 paths, we must remove node E.
*   To break Group 2 paths, we must remove either node P or node J.

Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.
A possible cut set is [E, J].
Another possible cut set is [E, P].

Let's verify the cut set [E, J]:
1.  Removing E breaks all paths B -> E -> M and B -> E -> L -> M.
2.  Removing J breaks all paths B -> ... -> P -> J -> M.
After removing E and J, M is only connected to L. B is connected to C, N, O, H. There is no path from B to L (since E is removed) or to M. Thus, B and M are disconnected.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, J]

1.  **Identify direct connections to H:** Node H is directly connected to B, G, and I. This means any path to H must use one of the edges (B, H), (G, H), or (I, H) as its final step.

2.  **Identify potential edge-disjoint paths:** To determine the minimum number of edges required, I look for edge-disjoint paths between B and H.
    *   **Path 1:** B - H (direct connection). This path uses the edge (B, H).
    *   **Path 2:** B - C - I - H. This path uses edges (B, C), (C, I), and (I, H).
    *   **Path 3:** B - E - F - G - H. This path uses edges (B, E), (E, F), (F, G), and (G, H).

3.  **Verify edge-disjoint nature:**
    *   Edges for Path 1: {(B, H)}
    *   Edges for Path 2: {(B, C), (C, I), (I, H)}
    *   Edges for Path 3: {(B, E), (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}
    These three sets of edges are distinct and share no common edges. Therefore, these three paths are edge-disjoint.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Menger's Theorem states that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges needed to disconnect them. Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 3.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects B and H. A straightforward approach is to remove all edges directly connected to H (excluding those that are part of B itself, which is not allowed).
    The edges connected to H are (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).
    If I remove these three edges:
    *   (B, H) breaks the direct path.
    *   (G, H) breaks all paths that reach H via G (e.g., B-E-F-G-H).
    *   (I, H) breaks all paths that reach H via I (e.g., B-C-I-H).
    After removing these three edges, node H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from B.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and I found a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum number of edges required.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

    *   Therefore, all paths originating from M must pass through either node E or node J.
        *   Paths starting M -> L will go M -> L -> E -> ...
        *   Paths starting M -> E will go M -> E -> ...
        *   Paths starting M -> J will go M -> J -> ...

5.  **Verify minimality:**

    *   Since no single node removal can disconnect M and H, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be 2 or more.

6.  **Conclusion:** The cut set {E, J} successfully disconnects M and H, and it is of size 2. Since a cut set of size 1 is not possible, 2 is the minimum number of nodes.

    *   **Path A:** M - E - H - B
        Edges: (M, E), (E, H), (H, B)

*   To isolate H, I must remove all edges connected to H. These are (H, G), (H, I), and (H, B). This would be a cut set of 3 edges.
*   To isolate B, I must remove all edges connected to B. These are (B, H), (B, E), (B, C), and (B, N). This would be a cut set of 4 edges.
Since 3 is less than 4, a cut of size 3 is possible: `[(H, G), (H, I), (H, B)]`.

**Strategy 2: Explore smaller cut sizes (1 or 2 edges).**

*   **Can it be 1 edge?**
    If I remove any single edge, for example, (H, B), there are still multiple paths remaining (e.g., H-G-F-E-B, H-I-C-B). So, 1 edge is not enough.

*   **Can it be 2 edges?**
    Let's consider two cases:
    *   **Case 2a: The direct edge (H, B) is one of the removed edges.**
        If (H, B) is removed, we still need to break all indirect paths. These paths branch out from H through G and I.
        - Paths through G: H-G-F-E-B, H-G-X-F-E-B.
        - Paths through I: H-I-C-B, H-I-P-O-C-B, H-I-P-O-N-B.

        - If the second edge is (H, I), paths through G (e.g., H-G-F-E-B) remain.
        - If the second edge is (E, B), paths through I (e.g., H-I-C-B) remain.

        Therefore, if (H, B) is removed, at least two more edges are needed to cut both the G-branch and the I-branch. This means a total of 1 + 2 = 3 edges are required in this case.

    *   **Case 2b: The direct edge (H, B) is NOT one of the removed edges.**
        In this scenario, the path H-B still exists. To disconnect H and B, we would need to remove at least 3 edges to cut all the indirect paths AND the direct path (H, B). This would mean a cut of at least 3 edges.
        Let's verify if we can cut all indirect paths with 2 edges, assuming (H,B) is not cut.

        - All paths from H through I eventually pass through (C, B) or (N, B).

        If we remove (E, B) and (N, B): The path H-I-C-B and H-I-P-O-C-B still exist.

-   If I remove C: Paths through B and O (G-...-B-N, G-...-O-N) remain.
-   If I remove O: Paths through B and C (G-...-B-N, G-...-C-N) remain.
-   If I remove I: Paths through B and M-J-P-O (G-...-B-N, G-...-M-J-P-O-N) remain.
-   If I remove P: Paths through B and C (G-...-B-N, G-...-C-N) remain.
-   If I remove M: Paths through H (G-...-H-B-N, G-...-H-I-C-N, G-...-H-I-P-O-N) remain.
-   Removing any other single node (K, X, L, J, D, Y) will not disconnect G and N, as they are peripheral or only affect a subset of paths.
Therefore, a cut set of size 1 is not possible.

**Let's try to find a cut set of size 2.**
Consider the nodes that act as "gateways" from G's side to the rest of the graph. These are F and H.
-   All paths from G must pass through F or H.
-   If we remove F, paths through H remain.
-   If we remove H, paths through F-E remain.

Let's consider the nodes H and E.
-   If H is removed, all paths that directly use H (G-H-B-N, G-H-I-C-N, G-H-I-P-O-N) are broken. However, paths that go through F-E and then M-J-P-O-N (G-F-E-M-J-P-O-N, G-F-E-L-M-J-P-O-N) are still active.
-   If E is removed, all paths that directly use E (G-F-E-M-J-P-O-N, G-F-E-L-M-J-P-O-N) are broken. However, paths that go through H (G-H-B-N, G-H-I-C-N, G-H-I-P-O-N) are still active.

Now, let's consider removing both H and E.
1.  **Paths through H:** If H is removed, all paths starting G-H-... are broken. This includes G-H-B-N, G-H-I-C-N, G-H-I-P-O-N.
2.  **Paths through F-E:** If E is removed, all paths starting G-F-E-... are broken. This includes G-F-E-H-B-N, G-F-E-H-I-C-N, G-F-E-H-I-P-O-N, G-F-E-M-J-P-O-N, G-F-E-L-M-J-P-O-N.

By removing both H and E, G becomes disconnected from the rest of the graph.
The nodes G, F, K, X form a connected component among themselves.
-   G is connected to F, K, X.
-   F is connected to G, K, X.
-   K is connected to G, F, X.
-   X is connected to G, F, K.
After removing H and E, none of these nodes (G, F, K, X) have any connections to any other node in the graph. Therefore, G is completely disconnected from N.

Since a cut set of size 1 is not possible, and we found a cut set of size 2, this is the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, E]

1.  **Identify all direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Node J is connected to: D, P, M, I.
    *   Node F is connected to: E, K, X, H.

2.  **Enumerate major path groups from J to F:**
    I observe that paths from J generally follow two main "arteries" or groups of paths to reach F:
    *   **Upper Path Group:** Paths that go from J through nodes like D, P, M, O, Y, N, B, L, and eventually reach F via node E.
    *   **Lower Path Group:** Paths that go from J through node I, then C or H, and eventually reach F via nodes H, X, K.

3.  **Analyze the Upper Path Group:**
    All paths in this group eventually converge on node E before reaching F via the edge (E,F).
    Examples:
    *   J - M - L - E - F
    *   J - M - O - B - E - F
    *   J - D - P - O - N - B - E - F
    *   J - P - Y - N - B - E - F
    *   J - I - C - B - E - F (This path also uses the upper group's final segment)
    Removing the edge **(E,F)** would disconnect all paths in this upper group from reaching F.

4.  **Analyze the Lower Path Group:**
    All paths in this group originate from J and pass through node I. From I, there are two main branches:
    *   Branch 1: J - I - C - ... (leading to H, X, K, and then F)
    *   Branch 2: J - I - H - ... (leading to X, G, K, and then F)
    To disconnect all paths originating from J-I, we need to cut both of these branches.
    *   Removing the edge **(I,C)** would disconnect all paths that go through J-I-C.
    *   Removing the edge **(I,H)** would disconnect all paths that go through J-I-H.

5.  **Formulate a potential cut set:**
    Based on the analysis, a potential cut set of 3 edges is: **{(E,F), (I,C), (I,H)}**.

6.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   **Edge (E,F):** This edge successfully cuts all paths from J that lead to F through node E. This includes paths like J-M-L-E-F, J-D-P-O-N-B-E-F, J-P-O-B-E-F, J-M-O-N-B-E-F, and any path that eventually reaches E and then F.
    *   **Edge (I,C):** This edge successfully cuts all paths from J that lead to F through the J-I-C branch. This includes paths like J-I-C-H-F, J-I-C-H-X-F, J-I-C-H-G-K-F, etc.
    *   **Edge (I,H):** This edge successfully cuts all paths from J that lead to F through the J-I-H branch. This includes paths like J-I-H-F, J-I-H-X-F, J-I-H-G-K-F, etc.

    Since all paths from J to F must pass through either E (and then (E,F)) or through I-C (and then (I,C)) or through I-H (and then (I,H)), removing these three edges will completely disconnect J from F.

7.  **Verify minimality:**
    To confirm that 3 is the minimum number of edges, I need to show that removing any two edges from the proposed cut set leaves at least one path between J and F.
    *   **Removing (E,F) and (I,C):** The path J-I-H-F still exists. (It is not cut by (E,F) as it doesn't use E, and not cut by (I,C) as it doesn't use C).

    *   **Removing (I,C) and (I,H):** The path J-M-L-E-F still exists. (It is not cut by (I,C) or (I,H) as it doesn't use I).

    Since removing any two edges from the set leaves a path, the set of 3 edges is indeed minimal.

2.  **Find edge-disjoint paths between H and B:**

    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** H - B
        Edges: {(H, B)}

    *   **Path 2:** H - G - B
        Edges: {(H, G), (G, B)}
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as it uses different edges.

    *   **Path 3:** H - I - C - B
        Edges: {(H, I), (I, C), (C, B)}
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 (no (H, B)) and Path 2 (no (H, G) or (G, B)). It uses (H, I), (I, C), and (C, B), which are not in Path 1 or Path 2.

    I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and B. This implies that at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect H from B.

3.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:**

    *   (H, I) is removed: Any path starting H-I-... is broken at the first step.

    The maximum number of edge-disjoint paths is 3, and I have found a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects H from B. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    *   Visited: `{J, D, P, M, O, I, E, L}`

    *   Dequeue `(I, [J, P, I])`. Neighbors of I are P (visited), C (visited), H.

        *   **Node F is the target! Path found: `[J, M, L, F]`. Length is 3.**

    *   Node D is connected to nodes J and P.
    *   Node E is connected to nodes L, M, F, and B.

2.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    A straightforward approach to disconnect two nodes is to isolate one of them by removing all its incident edges.
    *   To isolate node D, I would need to remove the edges (D, J) and (D, P). This would completely sever D from the rest of the graph, ensuring no path can reach it from E or any other node. This cut set has a size of 2.
    *   To isolate node E, I would need to remove the edges (E, L), (E, M), (E, F), and (E, B). This cut set has a size of 4.

3.  **Determine the minimum number of edges:**
    *   A cut set of size 2 (removing (D, J) and (D, P)) successfully disconnects D from E.
    *   Could a single edge removal disconnect D from E?

    *   Since a single edge removal is insufficient, the minimum number of edges must be at least 2.
    *   As I found a cut set of size 2 ([(D, J), (D, P)]), this is the minimum.

4.  **Verify the chosen cut set:**
    *   Removing (D, J) breaks all paths that would use J to reach D.
    *   Removing (D, P) breaks all paths that would use P to reach D.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are M and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect M from B. This is a minimum cut problem, which, by the Max-Flow Min-Cut theorem, is equivalent to finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between M and B.

First, I will try to identify edge-disjoint paths from M to B. Edge-disjoint means that no two paths share any common edges.

Let's list potential paths and try to select a set of mutually edge-disjoint paths:

1.  **Path 1: M - E - B**
    Edges used: `(M, E)`, `(E, B)`

2.  **Path 2: M - F - G - B**
    Edges used: `(M, F)`, `(F, G)`, `(G, B)`
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 (no common edges).

3.  **Path 3: M - J - I - H - B**
    Edges used: `(M, J)`, `(J, I)`, `(I, H)`, `(H, B)`
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2 (no common edges).

At this point, I have found 3 mutually edge-disjoint paths:
*   P1: M - E - B
*   P2: M - F - G - B
*   P3: M - J - I - H - B

Now, I will attempt to find a fourth edge-disjoint path.
Edges already used by P1, P2, P3:
`(M, E)`, `(E, B)`
`(M, F)`, `(F, G)`, `(G, B)`
`(M, J)`, `(J, I)`, `(I, H)`, `(H, B)`

Let's look for other paths from M to B using only unused edges:

*   **Paths starting with M - L:**
    *   M - L - E - B: `(E, B)` is used by P1. Not disjoint.
    *   M - L - E - G - B: `(G, B)` is used by P2. Not disjoint.

*   **Other paths starting with M - J:**
    *   M - J - I - C - B: `(M, J)` and `(J, I)` are used by P3. Not disjoint.
    *   M - J - P - O - C - B: `(M, J)` is used by P3. Not disjoint.
    *   M - J - P - O - N - B: `(M, J)` is used by P3. Not disjoint.

*   **Other paths starting with M - F:**
    *   M - F - K - G - B: `(M, F)` and `(G, B)` are used by P2. Not disjoint.
    *   M - F - X - K - G - B: `(M, F)` and `(G, B)` are used by P2. Not disjoint.

It appears that all other potential paths share at least one edge with one of the three paths (P1, P2, P3) identified above. This suggests that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths is 3.

According to the Max-Flow Min-Cut theorem, if the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths is 3, then the minimum number of edges to remove (the minimum cut) is also 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges whose removal completely disconnects M and B. A minimal cut set must contain at least one edge from each of the 3 edge-disjoint paths found.

Let's consider the following cut set: `[(E, B), (G, B), (M, J)]`

Let's verify if removing these three edges disconnects all paths from M to B:

1.  **Edge (E, B):** This edge cuts all paths that directly connect to B via E.
    *   M - E - B (cut)
    *   M - L - E - B (cut)

2.  **Edge (G, B):** This edge cuts all paths that directly connect to B via G.
    *   M - F - G - B (cut)
    *   M - F - K - G - B (cut)
    *   M - F - X - K - G - B (cut)
    *   M - E - G - B (cut)
    *   M - L - E - G - B (cut)

3.  **Edge (M, J):** This edge cuts all paths that originate from M through J.
    *   M - J - I - H - B (cut)
    *   M - J - I - C - B (cut)
    *   M - J - P - O - C - B (cut)
    *   M - J - P - O - N - B (cut)

By removing these three edges, all identified paths from M to B are broken. No path remains between M and B. Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects M and B, this is the minimum number of edges.

Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are H and O. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect H from O. I cannot remove H or O.

2.  **Analyze connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Nodes directly connected to H are G, I, C.
    *   Nodes directly connected to O are N, Y, P.

3.  **Identify potential bottlenecks:** Any path from H to O must pass through one of H's neighbors (G, I, C) and one of O's neighbors (N, Y, P). Let's focus on the nodes directly connected to O, as these are the "last points of contact" before reaching O.
    *   Node Y is connected only to N and O. If N is removed, Y becomes isolated from the rest of the graph (except O itself). This means that if N is removed, Y cannot serve as a bridge for any path from H to O.
    *   Therefore, all paths from H to O must ultimately pass through either node N or node P.

4.  **Test a cut set of size 1:**
    *   If I remove node N: Paths like H-G-F-P-O or H-I-P-O would still exist, as they go through P.
    *   If I remove node P: Paths like H-C-N-O or H-I-C-N-O would still exist, as they go through N.
    *   If I remove any other single node (e.g., G, I, C, F, B, E, etc.), there would still be paths through both N and P to O. For example, removing C would leave paths like H-G-F-P-O and H-I-P-O, and H-I-C-N-O would be broken, but H-I-P-O and H-G-F-P-O would still connect to O via P.
    *   Thus, a single node removal is not sufficient to disconnect H and O.

5.  **Test a cut set of size 2:**
    *   Based on the observation that all paths must pass through either N or P, let's consider removing both N and P.
    *   If **N is removed**:
        *   All paths that use N to reach O (e.g., H-C-N-O, H-I-C-N-O) are broken.
        *   Node Y, which was connected to N and O, is now only connected to O. Since N is removed, H can no longer reach Y (and thus O via Y).
    *   If **P is removed**:
        *   All paths that use P to reach O (e.g., H-G-F-P-O, H-I-P-O, H-C-B-E-P-O) are broken.
    *   If **both N and P are removed**:
        *   All paths to O via N are broken.
        *   All paths to O via P are broken.
        *   Since Y's only connection to the rest of the graph (apart from O) was N, and N is removed, Y is also effectively disconnected from H.
        *   Therefore, H and O are completely disconnected.

6.  **Confirm minimality:** Since a single node removal was insufficient, and a two-node removal (N, P) successfully disconnects H and O, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [N, P]

============================================================
Image name: SEIS_task2_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 15
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  Identify the two black target nodes: E and H. The goal is to disconnect E and H by removing the minimum number of other nodes.
2.  To find the minimum number of nodes to remove, I will look for node-disjoint paths between E and H. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes in a cut set (excluding the source and target) is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between the source and target.
3.  Let's systematically search for node-disjoint paths from E to H:
    *   **Path 1:** E - G - H
        *   This path uses node G.
    *   **Path 2:** E - B - H
        *   This path uses node B. It is node-disjoint from Path 1 (except for E and H), as it uses B while Path 1 uses G.
    *   **Path 3:** Now I need to find a path that does not use G or B.
        *   From E, I can go to L or F.
        *   If I go E - F, F is connected to G, K, X. All paths from F eventually lead to G (F-G-H, F-K-G-H, F-X-K-G-H). Since G is used by Path 1, paths through F are not node-disjoint from Path 1.
        *   If I go E - L:
            *   E - L - M
            *   From M, I can go to J or F. F leads to G, which is already used. So, I must go M - J.
            *   E - L - M - J
            *   From J, I can go to P or D. D is a dead end for H. So, I must go J - P.
            *   E - L - M - J - P
            *   From P, I can go to I or O.
                *   If I go P - O, O is connected to C and Y. C is connected to I, B, N. Y is connected to N, O. N is connected to B, C, Y. All these paths eventually lead to H via I, B, or G. Since B and G are already used by Path 1 and Path 2, I need to ensure I is not used by this path if possible, or that this path is distinct.
                *   If I go P - I, I is connected to H.
            *   So, **Path 3:** E - L - M - J - P - I - H.
        *   Let's check if Path 3 (E-L-M-J-P-I-H) is node-disjoint from Path 1 (E-G-H) and Path 2 (E-B-H).
            *   Path 1 uses G.
            *   Path 2 uses B.
            *   Path 3 uses L, M, J, P, I.
            *   None of the internal nodes (G, B, L, M, J, P, I) are shared between these three paths. Therefore, these are three node-disjoint paths.

4.  Since I have found 3 node-disjoint paths between E and H, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect them is 3.

5.  To form a cut set, I need to select one node from each of these node-disjoint paths (excluding E and H). A straightforward choice is to pick the immediate neighbors of H that are used by these paths.
    *   From Path 1: G
    *   From Path 2: B
    *   From Path 3: I (or P, J, M, L)
    *   So, a cut set is {G, B, I}.

6.  Verify that removing {G, B, I} disconnects E and H:
    *   H's only direct neighbors are G, I, and B.
    *   If G, I, and B are all removed, H will have no remaining connections to any other node in the graph.
    *   Therefore, no path can exist between E and H.

7.  Since we found 3 node-disjoint paths, and we identified a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum possible number of nodes to remove.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [G, B, I]

    *   Any path starting E-H directly passes through H.

    *   Removing M breaks all paths that use M (e.g., E-M-J-I, E-M-J-P-I).

    *   If I remove K, paths like E-B-H-I-J still exist.
    *   No single node can disconnect E and J. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 2.

        *   If a path from E to J does *not* go through B, it must start from E and go through L, M, or F (e.g., E-F-K-J). As observed in step 2, all such paths eventually pass through K to reach J. Removing K will break all these paths.

        *   M is connected only to E and L (its connection to F is removed).
        *   Neither L nor M are connected to any other part of the graph that could lead to J.
        *   Thus, E becomes completely isolated from J. This is also a valid cut set of size 2.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since a cut set of size 1 is not possible, and we have found multiple cut sets of size 2, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

*   Neighbors of G: H, K, F, X
*   Neighbors of I: H, P, J, C

Next, I systematically explore all possible paths from G to I. I'll group them by the first step from G and the last step to I.

**Paths from G to I:**

1.  **Paths starting G -> H:**
    *   G - H - I (Direct path)
    *   G - H - C - I (Path through C)

2.  **Paths starting G -> F:**
    *   G - F - P - I
    *   G - F - P - J - I

3.  **Paths starting G -> K:**
    *   G - K - F - P - I
    *   G - K - F - P - J - I
    *   (Note: K is connected to F and X, so paths through K will eventually lead to F)

4.  **Paths starting G -> X:**
    *   G - X - F - P - I
    *   G - X - F - P - J - I
    *   (Note: X is connected to F, so paths through X will eventually lead to F)

From this analysis, it's clear that all paths from G to I must pass through one of two main "channels":
*   **Channel 1:** Through node H (which can then lead directly to I or via C to I).
*   **Channel 2:** Through node F (which can be reached directly from G, or via K or X, and then leads to P, which then leads to I or via J to I).

To disconnect G from I, I need to cut both of these channels.

**Strategy:** Identify a minimal set of edges that cuts all paths in Channel 1 and all paths in Channel 2.

**Cutting Channel 1 (paths through H):**
The paths in this channel are G - H - I and G - H - C - I.
If I remove the edge (G, H):
*   G can no longer reach H.
*   Therefore, the path G - H - I is broken.
*   Also, since G cannot reach H, and H is the only way G can reach C (verified by inspecting connections of C: N, B, H, I, O; and G: H, K, F, X - no other connection from G to N, B, O), G cannot reach C.
*   Therefore, the path G - H - C - I is also broken.
*   So, removing (G, H) effectively cuts off all paths from G to I that rely on H or C.

**Cutting Channel 2 (paths through F):**
The paths in this channel are G - F - P - I, G - F - P - J - I, and all variations starting with G - K - F... or G - X - F....
All these paths converge at node F and then proceed to P.
If I remove the edge (F, P):
*   G (and K, X) can no longer reach P via F.
*   Can G reach P through any other way? (G,H) is already cut. P is connected to F, J, I, O, E. G cannot reach O or E without H or F. So, G cannot reach P at all.
*   Therefore, the paths G - F - P - I, G - K - F - P - I, G - X - F - P - I are all broken.
*   Similarly, since G cannot reach P, it cannot reach J via P. Can G reach J through any other way? J is connected to P, D, M, I. G cannot reach D or M. So, G cannot reach J at all.
*   Therefore, the paths G - F - P - J - I, G - K - F - P - J - I, G - X - F - P - J - I are all broken.
*   So, removing (F, P) effectively cuts off all paths from G to I that rely on F, P, or J.

**Conclusion:**
By removing the two edges (G, H) and (F, P), all identified paths from G to I are broken.
*   (G, H) disconnects G from H and C.
*   (F, P) disconnects G (via F, K, X) from P and J.

Since all paths to I must pass through either {H, C} or {P, J}, and both sets of paths are disconnected from G by these two edge removals, G and I are completely disconnected.

**Minimum Number of Edges:**
To confirm this is the minimum, I consider if a single edge removal would suffice.
*   Removing (G, H) would leave paths like G - F - P - I.
*   Removing (F, P) would leave paths like G - H - I.
Since there are at least two independent "branches" of paths (one through H, one through F), at least two edges must be removed.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(G, H), (F, P)]

2.  **Look for edge-disjoint paths.**
    To determine the minimum number of edges to remove (the min-cut), I can use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges in an edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

    Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path A:** H - B - N
        Edges: {(H, B), (B, N)}
    *   **Path B:** H - I - C - N
        Edges: {(H, I), (I, C), (C, N)}

    *   **Path C:** H - E - M - J - P - O - N
        Edges: {(H, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)}
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path A (H-B-N) and Path B (H-I-C-N).

    Since I have found three edge-disjoint paths (Path A, Path B, and Path C), according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and N is at least 3.

3.  **Find a cut set of size 3.**
    Now that I know the minimum is at least 3, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects H and N.

    Consider the edges directly connected to node N, excluding (Y, N) since Y is only connected to N and O, and any path from H to Y would have to pass through N or O first.
    The edges connecting N to the rest of the graph (from H's perspective) are (B, N), (C, N), and (O, N).

    *   Removing (B, N) breaks Path A (H - B - N).
    *   Removing (C, N) breaks Path B (H - I - C - N).
    *   Removing (O, N) breaks Path C (H - E - M - J - P - O - N). It also breaks Path 3 (H - I - P - O - N) and Path 5 (H - G - F - E - M - J - P - O - N), and any other path that eventually reaches N via O.

No path from H can reach Y without first passing through N or O (which is now disconnected from N). Therefore, H is completely disconnected from N.

This cut set of 3 edges successfully disconnects H from N, and since we established that at least 3 edges are required, this is the minimum.

    All intermediate edges are distinct from each other and from the edges connected to H and N.

Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and N is at least 3.

Next, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that achieves this disconnection. A common strategy is to consider the edges directly connected to one of the target nodes. Node N is connected to B, C, O, and Y. Let's consider removing three of these edges:

**Proposed Cut Set: [(B,N), (C,N), (O,N)]**

Now, I will verify if removing these three edges completely disconnects H from N:
*   **Path H - B - N:** This path is broken by the removal of edge (B,N).
*   **Path H - I - C - N:** This path is broken by the removal of edge (C,N).
*   **Path H - I - P - O - N:** This path is broken by the removal of edge (O,N).
*   **Path H - I - J - P - O - N:** This path is broken by the removal of edge (O,N).
*   **Path H - I - E - B - N:** This path is broken by the removal of edge (B,N).
*   **Path H - F - E - B - N:** This path is broken by the removal of edge (B,N).
*   **Path H - F - E - I - C - N:** This path is broken by the removal of edge (C,N).
*   **Path H - F - E - I - P - O - N:** This path is broken by the removal of edge (O,N).
*   **Path H - F - E - M - J - P - O - N:** This path is broken by the removal of edge (O,N).
*   Any other path from H to N must eventually reach N through one of its direct neighbors (B, C, O, Y). With (B,N), (C,N), and (O,N) removed, the only remaining direct connection to N is via (Y,N).
*   Therefore, for H to reach N, it must reach Y. Let's check if H can reach Y.
    *   Node Y is connected only to N and O.
    *   Since (O,N) has been removed, O is no longer connected to N.
    *   Thus, Y is only connected to N.
    *   If N is only connected to Y, and Y is only connected to N, then N and Y form an isolated component, disconnected from the rest of the graph, including H.

Since all paths from H to N are broken by removing the edges (B,N), (C,N), and (O,N), and this cut set has a size of 3, which matches the minimum determined by Menger's Theorem, this is the minimum number of edges to remove.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, N), (C, N), (O, N)]

        *   Intermediate nodes: {X, K, J, P}
    *   **Path 3:** G - F - E - B - C - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {F, E, B, C}

3.  **Verify node-disjointness:**
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 1 is {H}.
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 2 is {X, K, J, P}.
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 3 is {F, E, B, C}.
    These three sets of intermediate nodes are mutually disjoint. This means we have found 3 node-disjoint paths between G and I.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect two nodes (excluding the source and target nodes) is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between them. Since we found 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3.

5.  **Propose a cut set of size 3:** Based on the identified paths, a cut set could be formed by picking one node from each of these disjoint paths. For example, let's consider removing H from Path 1, J from Path 2, and E from Path 3. So, the proposed cut set is [H, J, E].

6.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   **Removing H:** This immediately breaks Path 1 (G - H - I).
    *   **Removing J:** This breaks all paths that go through J to reach I, such as Path 2 (G - X - K - J - P - I) and any other path like G - X - F - E - L - M - J - P - I.
    *   **Removing E:** This breaks all paths that go through E to reach I, such as Path 3 (G - F - E - B - C - I) and any other path like G - X - F - E - B - C - I.

    Let's confirm that no other paths remain. The nodes directly connected to I are H, P, and C.
    *   H is removed, so G cannot reach I via H.
    *   Can G reach P? P is connected to J, I, D, O. Since J is removed, G cannot reach P through J. D and O are not connected to G's side of the graph. So, G cannot reach P.
    *   Can G reach C? C is connected to I, B, N, O, P. P is unreachable. B is connected to E, H, C, N. Since E and H are removed, G cannot reach B. N is connected to B, C, O. Since B is unreachable, G cannot reach N. O is not connected to G's side. So, G cannot reach C.

    Since G cannot reach H, P, or C, it cannot reach I. Therefore, removing nodes [H, J, E] successfully disconnects G from I.

7.  **Conclusion:** We have identified 3 node-disjoint paths, which means at least 3 nodes must be removed. We have also found a cut set of size 3 ([H, J, E]) that successfully disconnects G and I. Thus, 3 is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [H, J, E]

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect H from B. This is equivalent to finding the minimum edge cut between H and B. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

First, I will try to find a set of edge-disjoint paths from H to B. If I can find 'k' edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut must be at least 'k'.

Let's identify paths from H to B:
1.  **Path 1 (P1): H - I - C - B**
    Edges: (H, I), (I, C), (C, B)

2.  **Path 2 (P2): H - X - K - F - E - B**
    Edges: (H, X), (X, K), (K, F), (F, E), (E, B)

3.  Now, I need to find a third path that does not share any edges with P1 or P2.
    P1 edges: {(H, I), (I, C), (C, B)}
    P2 edges: {(H, X), (X, K), (K, F), (F, E), (E, B)}

    Let's try a path starting with (H, G):
    **Path 3 (P3): H - G - I - J - P - O - B**
    Edges: (H, G), (G, I), (I, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, B)

    Let's verify if P3 is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2:
    *   (H, G) is not in P1 or P2.
    *   (G, I) is not in P1 or P2.
    *   (I, J) is not in P1 or P2. (I, C) is in P1, but (I, J) is distinct.
    *   (J, P) is not in P1 or P2.
    *   (P, O) is not in P1 or P2.
    *   (O, B) is not in P1 or P2.

    All edges in P1, P2, and P3 are distinct. Therefore, I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and B.
    This implies that the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and B is at least 3.

Next, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that actually disconnects H from B.
A straightforward way to disconnect a node from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges directly connected to it.
Node H is connected to three other nodes: I, X, and G.
The edges connected to H are: (H, I), (H, X), and (H, G).

If I remove these three edges:
*   (H, I) is removed.
*   (H, X) is removed.
*   (H, G) is removed.

After removing these edges, node H has no remaining connections to any other node in the graph. Consequently, no path can exist from H to B. This is a valid cut set of size 3.

Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths (proving the minimum cut is at least 3) and I have found a cut set of size 3, this is indeed the minimum number of edges to remove.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are N and G. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect N from G. I cannot remove N or G.

First, I will try to find if a single node removal can disconnect N and G.
Let's consider the direct neighbors of N: B, C, Y.
Let's consider the direct neighbors of G: H, K, X.

If I remove B: Paths like N-C-I-H-G still exist.
If I remove C: Paths like N-B-H-G still exist.
If I remove Y: Paths like N-B-H-G and N-C-I-H-G still exist.
If I remove H: Paths like N-B-E-F-K-G still exist.
If I remove K: Paths like N-B-H-G still exist.
If I remove X: Paths like N-B-H-G still exist.
No single node removal disconnects N from G. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 2.

Now, I will look for a cut set of size 2.
I need to identify two nodes that, if removed, would break all paths between N and G.

Let's analyze the structure of paths from N to G.
All paths from N to G can be broadly categorized into two main "channels" or "bottlenecks":
1.  Paths that go through node H to reach G.
2.  Paths that go through nodes F, K, or X to reach G. These paths must pass through F, and F's only connection to the "left" side of the graph (where N is) is through node E.

Let's test removing nodes H and E:

**If H is removed:**
Any path that directly uses H to reach G (e.g., N-B-H-G, N-C-I-H-G, N-Y-O-P-I-H-G) is broken.
G can still be reached via K or X. Both K and X are connected to F. So, any remaining path to G must pass through F.

**If E is also removed (along with H):**
Now, consider paths that would reach G via F (and then K or X).
The node F is connected to E, K, and X. Since E is removed, F is only connected to K and X.
To reach F from N, a path must have gone through E.
Let's trace paths from N to F:
*   N -> B -> E (removed) -> F. (Broken)
*   N -> C -> I -> P -> J -> M -> E (removed) -> F. (Broken)
*   N -> C -> O -> P -> J -> M -> E (removed) -> F. (Broken)
*   N -> Y -> O -> P -> J -> M -> E (removed) -> F. (Broken)
*   N -> B -> L -> E (removed) -> F. (Broken)
*   N -> B -> M -> E (removed) -> F. (Broken)

Since E is removed, there is no way to reach F from N. Consequently, there is no way to reach K or X (which are connected to F) from N. This means G cannot be reached via K or X.

**Conclusion after removing H and E:**
*   All paths that went through H are broken because H is removed.
*   All paths that went through E (to reach F, K, or X) are broken because E is removed.
*   Since all paths from N to G must pass through either H or E (to reach F, K, X), removing both H and E disconnects N from G.

**Verification of minimality:**
As established earlier, a single node removal is not sufficient. Therefore, a cut set of size 2 is minimal.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, E]

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths from F to J:
    *   **Path 1 (P1): F - K - M - J**
        Edges: (F, K), (K, M), (M, J)
    *   **Path 2 (P2): F - E - B - C - I - J**
